001 Introduction.en
===================

Hello, everyone, and welcome to this natural language processing course. Thank you for enrolling in this course. I hope you're excited to learn about all these new concepts about NLP, short for natural language processing. So here we are going to use Python as our coding language. A bit of context about myself: I'm a data scientist here in Portugal, based in Lisbon, Portugal, and I have around eight years of experience in data science related roles.

I've studied more on the ETL and the engineering side of things, and then I've moved to data science, and now I am a partner at DareData Engineering, which is a Portuguese startup focused on implementing machine learning and data science systems. Regarding natural language processing, I have implemented a couple of systems in large corporate customers, and also my master thesis was focused on text classification. Regarding sectors, I work mostly in retail and banking, and as a consultant, something that I do today, working for several customers, supporting them in their data initiatives.

So what were my main motivations for creating this course? First, I wanted to help introduce natural language processing for beginners. Natural language processing is a huge field and it has a lot of concepts attached to it. I've decided to provide this course for beginners because I think that these fundamentals that we will talk about will not go away in the next 10 or 20 years. I believe they are the fundamentals of natural language processing for the future.

And so they will give you a good base if you want to investigate further other natural language processing courses that have more advanced techniques or are more specialized in a subset of NLP techniques. And NLP is one of the most exciting subjects nowadays in data science, and the scientific advancements that have been done in this field particularly [unclear] forward a lot of initiatives such as text processing, translation, information retrieval and sentiment analysis or text classification.

Another motivation is that I want to help entry-level data scientists to understand what may be a future career path, because natural language processing is really funneling towards a specific career path for data scientists. There are people that are more generalist like I am, because I tend to do projects that have some concepts of NLP, have some concepts of more traditional data science, have some components of data engineering. But really, you can be an expert if you want.

And these fundamentals that we will study will also help you to understand if that's the path that you want to go forward. So here is my LinkedIn and also the DareData website. I'll leave them in the resources if you want to connect with me on LinkedIn or just check the website of my company. A bit about the course: I designed this course for beginners. First, we will start with a Python quick course that will help you to understand the fundamentals of the Python language.

So it will be just a crash course for you not to get lost around all the other sections, because we'll have [unclear] Python in this course. So this course will help you if you don't have any experience. If you have, you can skip this section. Then we are going to work with strings in Python. Strings, or characters, are really the raw material of natural language processing. And we will deal with the most important functions that we can call on strings, because we really have to master working with characters and strings if we want to be confident when working with text data.

After that, we'll spend a lot of time understanding the Natural Language Toolkit library, which is one of the most important libraries in Python regarding natural language processing. So the Natural Language Toolkit library already has a lot of functions and models pre-developed that we can use to understand a bit more about advanced concepts in NLP. We'll spend a lot of time understanding some of the most important functions of this library. Then, as most of the text that you will work on throughout your life as a data scientist or as an NLP practitioner will not come to you automatically, we'll understand a couple of things about gathering text data, such as reading data from websites using scrapers or reading data from external files. Then we'll approach an exciting concept, which is word vectors, which are one of the most important concepts in NLP and were a huge scientific advancement in this field.

In the past decade. We'll explore simple word vectors using co-occurrence matrices, and then we'll develop our own implementation of word vectors using a neural network, which is a more advanced model, and it's tied to the paper that was a breakthrough in this field in 2013. Finally, we are going to look at text representation, or how we can represent sentences as vectors and as mathematical representations, and look at text classification. Depending on the time that you will enroll in this course,

the last section, text classification, may already be available, because it's still in development, and also more sections might be available because I will be always updating this course with new material based on students' feedback. So how are you going to proceed in the course? Well, three important things. We'll have the long lectures where you will see me coding, and I expect that you follow along with the code on your system. Then you'll have quizzes about the things that we have learned in that section.

And finally, you'll have practical exercises to test the knowledge and intuition behind everything that we've studied so far. And most importantly, by the end of the course, you should be able to classify your own text. For example, classifying the part-of-speech tags in a specific text, being able to apply a POS tagger to a sentence to extract nouns, verbs or adjectives in a specific text. You will also understand the concept behind word vectors, and you'll be able to calculate your own word similarities.

Additionally, you will be able to extract text from a web page. For example, here we have a Wikipedia page that we will learn to extract directly into Python using scrapers. And finally, you will be able to build your own text pipeline, or your data pipeline based on textual data. These are some of the concepts that we will learn. But of course, we will learn many more, such as stemming, lemmatization, the concept of stop words, and text classification, as examples.

So now move forward to set up your environment. Check on the resources, the course materials. You'll be able to access all the course materials that you need for this course. You'll not need to pay anything to access these materials, of course. So this is all in Python, an open-source language, and just set up your environment to be able to go along in this lecture. So, again, thank you for enrolling in this course, and I'm excited to have you on board.

We can perform mathematical operations in this data. And then we have the counterpart with the decimal point that's called the floats. The floats have these decimal point.

Basically, they are something like fractional numbers. Of course, they can also be integers. If you have 1.0, that will be considered the float. But mostly they are used for fractional numbers or fractional variables, such as one divided by two, which is 0.5. That will normally yield a float, which is this data type, a bit different from integer because it supports all those tiny values between, for example, zero and one. And we can perform also mathematical operations with them.

Of course, they are numbers just like any other number. And then we have one of the most important data types for this course, which are the strings or characters which are text, we are dealing with the natural language processing course here. This data type is extremely important because it's one of the raw materials of natural language processing. Of course, we cannot perform mathematical operations with them because they are characters. So let's see the basics.

OK, let's enter another realm of python objects, which may cause some confusion in the beginning, and the first Python object that we are going to explore is Python lists. I'm just exemplifying what a list is. So all this is initiated using the square brackets and then you can pass the elements specifically inside list.

And in this case, this is a list that's called my list. Again, I'm doing an assignment. So these assignments are pretty cool because instead of having to type this list every time I wanted to do something with it, I can use this my_list name, which is, of course, much more practical and noticed now that we have here one, two, three, four, five, six elements inside this list. So we have created a list with six elements of integer type. Now, an important thing is that lists can be multi type.

You can have integers mixed with strings mixed with floats. Python will not convert this list to any other thing, will not convert the elements according to a specific type. So that's also pretty cool. And you can use this flexibility on lists to do many things. Another important concept in Python is that indexes start on zero. So think of it as an elevator. So elevator starts on zero. So the Python indexes. Here in the my_list object, which is a list, I can call with these square brackets the zero index and this will return the element one because it's this one.

So zero goes for the first position in my list. If I have one, this will go for the 20. In this case, the number 20, because that's the second element. And recall that every time you are accessing an element from a list, you will use this square brackets here with the index position of the element that you want to extract. I see another example. 006 Escaping Characters.en
==========================

Hi, everyone, and welcome again to this section on working with strings, so in this section, we are going to talk a bit about escaping characters and common errors that will come when you work with strings in Python. So one of the things that you will probably deal with a lot is the following. Imagine that we would have the following sentence, which is this is someone's jacket. Look at something a bit weird, which is this text is highlighted [unclear] and this text is applied as black.

This has some meaning. It means that Python is only treating "this is someone" as text. It's not treating this "s jacket" as text, although this is not a real apostrophe. Sometimes you would see it written this way. So let's try to run this sentence and see what we will have. We'll have an error because Python can't interpret this "s jacket", thinking that this is some code that should be executed, but this shouldn't be interpreted, this code. So one of the most easy ways to go around this is to have an escaping character.

So the escaping character is this bar leaning to the left that will escape the next character, making it being treated as a string, specifically as a string and not as something inherently to the python language. That means that this apostrophe will no longer be used with its function of opening or closing strings. It will be used as pure text and having this bar leaning to the left before everything will escape that character. This is the meaning of escaping that character means this character will no longer have its function on the Python language like equals, for example, has a specific function like this bar that has that specific function of escaping characters.

Having this bar will escape the next character and you can also escape that by itself, as we will see in the future. But here, notice how as soon as I put this bar, all the text is red. That means that now everything is being interpreted as string. Let's see if I print this sentence. The magic thing is that that bar disappears because as it is being used as an escaping character, it will not be printed as part of the text because it's just being used on the definition of the sentence.

And it's not here. We have the correct string or the correct sentence when we print this sentence. Even with that character in there, of course, there are more things that you can do, for example, using different quotes. Let's see if I use this one, which is instead of using a single quote, we use double quotes and then Python will only close the string when it finds another double quote. And in that case, we can run this and we see that the sentence is OK.

Now, this is not super common because most of the times you will not define your text. As we will see throughout the course, you will read text from other systems, from websites, from different sources that are not related to you writing the text on your own. That's extremely rare, I would say. So this is more kind of a workaround, not probably a solution that will stick with you a lot of times depending on your applications and what you'll do with the string, you might do it.

Even so, it's not something super common and it's best that you understand the concept of escaping characters. So let's now define something super interesting that we will use, for example, when reading files, which is imagine that I have this path in my computer and you know that paths in computers start something like this. So C, colon, slash, Users, slash, my name, Ivo, for example. And now I can run this one and remember that I have a problem here because we have these bars leaning to the left that should escape.

characters. What Python is telling me is that these bars are being used for something else. I can escape the bars themselves by putting two bars. So a bar before the bar. And now if I check it, I'll have my path. Because I've escaped the purpose of this of this bar on the string, there's another way for us to make the whole text escape itself by using raw strings, raw strings, make all the text inside, being treated as text and ignoring all the parts of where it can be interpreted as something that has a function in Python code.

Let's see how we can define a string. For example, if I remove these bars again, I would have the error for the path defining a raw string. It's as simple as using an r before these single quotes, or the double quotes as you wish. But before defining the string you use this r and it will read everything inside as a raw string. Even so, it can't deal with this escape right here that we have in the end. So this is something that sometimes using a raw string may lead to some errors, but it's another technique that you can use to escape some characters.

The concept is that sometimes when we define strings, we have to pay attention with the characters that we have inside because those characters are reserved to do something in the Python language. And depending on how reserve they are and what they do, we have to pay attention to them and escape them in some way with these errors. So having these bars that escape the next character in the sentence or having this raw string that depending on the application or the text that we have, may work on defining our string or not give us errors on the definition.

001 [Slides] - NLTK Intro and Tokenizers.en
===========================================

Hello and welcome again to this natural language processing course, so here we are going to introduce some specific concept of natural language processing, in this case, the Natural Language Toolkit, NLTK. So the Natural Language Toolkit is one of the fundamentals of NLP in Python. It's not specifically good in doing anything, but it contains the most basic elements for you to proceed, with your pipeline in a good and practical manner.

Also, this library is really used to explain most of the concepts that we use in NLP, such as text normalization, such as tokenization, such as part-of-speech tagging and other concepts that we will explore here. Again, this is one of the oldest libraries used in NLP, and it contains a lot of methods that we can use and that are extremely important for us to understand how most of the techniques work. So small intro Natural Language Tool Kit was released in 2001.

It was a project by the Department of Computer and Information Science from the University of Pennsylvania. Most important features of this library is that we have POS tagging, for parts of speech. We have tokenization, we have stemming and lemmatization. Both of these are text normalization techniques. Let's start with tokenization and understanding of what we commonly call sentence tokenization, so the sentence tokenization means that when we have a specific text like this, this is extremely common in NLP.

We want to chunk this text into sentences. And for us as humans, it's kind of simple to do it just by reading the text, seeing the full stops. And we know that that sentence should stop there. For the computer, This may be a bit difficult and we'll see why in the practical lectures. But one of the most common issues when you do, for example, the splitting on the full stop is that you may find somewhere in your text a point that's being used as a decimal place for a number and that would basically ruin your approach.

And that's why you have this sentence tokenization available for you in NLTK, ready to use and ready to fit or ready to pass your text through the sentence tokenization to obtain the sentences separated in the text. Now, this is what we commonly call tokenization. Tokenization is a way of saying that we are dividing our text into smaller chunks in some way. In this case, we are producing sentence tokens where the sentence tokens altogether make our text.

And one of the most important things right now is that we are not going to train most of our models. So here we are just going to pick up the models that are off the shelf available for us because we don't get much gain on training our own sentence tokenizer, as this is of course, on the fundamentals. I think that just funneling on one of those concepts would take us, for example, four or five hours and we wouldn't have the return of you getting a broad view of everything regarding natural language processing.

So we are going to train our own models, of course, for example, in the word vectors and in the text classification. But here for these models, it doesn't make much sense to train our models. It's just important that you understand how these functions work on a high level here in the NLTK section we'll train our own POS tagger, but that will be just for exemplification and not to obtain a better performance of the ones that we have already available in NLTK, because those ones are pretty good and they have good performance when you apply them to your text.

So, for example, the one that we use in sentence tokenization as of the date where I'm recording this course, which is 2021, this uses an instance of a Punkt sentence tokenizer, which is a kind of model that checks the context of the sentence to understand how the punctuation works around in a sentence and where it should split the sentence when it applies its tokenizer. This is an instance of this sentence tokenizer that people already pre-trained on other text that we don't have available.

But we can pick up this trained model and apply it to our own text. We understand what training a model means throughout the course. From sentences, we can also go into smaller chunks of our text, which are the words, so we can do word tokenization, which is, for example, here in the sentence, Portugal would be word one, Portuguese here would be word two, until word n. And this is just a small paragraph and it doesn't even end in a small paragraph.

But you can split this text in several ways. We are also going to use a pre-trained version here on word tokenization that's already available for us from NLTK. There are several tokenizers that you can think of. One of the most simple ones is the white space based, that basically splits our sentence by the white spaces that sometimes may not be the best approach. And we'll see why during the practical lectures. Then we have punctuation based, which I think is a bit too aggressive because it takes out, for example, the apostrophes from the word might not be what we want for our text.

And then we have the default Treebank tokenizer, which is the one that we will use. That also takes into account the context of the word to split the sentence. We will see that with more detail in the practical lectures. We also have another one. We have here, for example, the tweet tokenizer that basically keeps the hashtags together because the hashtags on Twitter have their own meaning.

2.  

002 [Slides] - Text Normalization Techniques.en
===============================================

OK, so tokenization, we've covered those concepts, at least on a higher level. This is extremely important because most of the applications pass through some kind of tokenization, as you will see. So this is normally one of the first steps that you do when you apply in an NLP pipeline, at least in most of the tools. So let's proceed with normalization and what normalization means in this case in text. So, for example, stemming is a text normalization technique that helps you to reduce your words to similar prefixes.

For example, creating will be treated as creat, creation again will be treated as creat, create will also be treated as creat. And we have different stemmers: Porter, Snowball and Lancaster. The Porter and Snowball are kind of similar in terms of the aggressiveness of how they cut the word to their prefix. But Snowball is a more advanced version of Porter. Lancaster is really aggressive and it tends to overstem, which means that it cuts your words more aggressively than the others.

Again, we'll explore all of this during the practical lectures. You also have an article here that I'll leave in the resources, which is an article that I wrote and that you can access for free if you want to understand a bit more how stemming works in the background. It's just important that you understand the difference between the Porter, Snowball and Lancaster. Positive side of stemming: It reduces your vocabulary. You'll understand this also why this is important.

It saves space. Basically, you treat your words as smaller elements and you convey the meaning of the words between different conjugations. For example, between creation and creating. You convey the meaning of create for the semantics of the words creat, which would otherwise be considered different, as we've seen in the past section where these words are completely different from a computer, it doesn't even have the same meaning or morphological meaning, if I can say it in this way, for a computer.

So it's also one of the steps that enables a computer to treat both of these words as the same, or at least with the same type of meaning on the outside. Of course, most of these techniques have a downside. We lose information when we apply this normalization, as with any other normalization technique. For example, in numerical data, you can use the principal component analysis. If you are aware of that or if you have worked before with that type of data, you lose information from the original matrix, as we will see during the course.

Another technique that's commonly used for normalization is lemmatization. But lemmatization uses the context, and not only the prefix of the word, to create what we usually call the lemmas, which are the results of applying then our lemmatization. For example, the word is: there is no prefix [unclear] that would output be. But for lemmatization, you can do it because lemmatization understands that be is a conjugation of is. And are, that's exactly the same. You would have the be. And also for the word be.

Of course, the lemmatization, or the lemmatized version, is be, so three different conjugations of the verb would have exactly or be considered the same verb. Now how does exactly lemmatization do this? Well, it needs to have a part of speech tag that will speak about next. We'll get there in a minute. But the positive: it also reduces your vocabulary, saves some space. On the downside, you lose information and it needs parts of speech. So you need to give the function of that word in that specific text.

OK, now that I spoke about part of speech, it's important for us to understand what really is part of speech. Well, part of speech is a way for us to tag our sentence and give our words, the function or the objective that they have in that sentence. This is a commonly used example. For example, she sells seashells on the seashore and you have she is a personal pronoun. Sells is a verb in the present tense. Seashells is a plural noun, on is a preposition,

the is a determinant, and seashore is a singular noun. All of these words have their function on the sentence and that's how you understand how they relate to each other based on this part of speech. So the part-of-speech tags are really these tags right here that indicates the function of each word in a sentence. So we'll explore this concept in two ways. Well, again, the pre-trained model from NLTK, but we'll also train our own POS tagger based on a simple probabilistic model, which is really simple.

And I'll explain you in a bit. So the model is actually really interesting. It has a couple of variables regarding the context of the word. For example, what words precede that specific word? How many words are in the context of that word and other characteristics of the word in the sentence? They apply weights to those variables to obtain a final part of speech tag for that word. This may seem confusing for you at the moment, but we'll explore this concept in more detail in the word vector part, particularly neural networks.

This one is a really simple neural network that only has here [unclear]. This is what you call here an output. This doesn't have even hidden layers. So if neural networks have hidden layers, that's actually the origin of the term deep learning, which you might have heard of. But even so, this is just a simple neural network. Now that I spoke about, the accuracy will also talk about that. But imagine that, for example, what this model will do is that each time it gets a sentence, it will check the word. And based on the context of that word, which should be those variables right here. So I think there are like between 10 and 20. I'm not really sure about that. Variables that are considered for this simple perceptron. I'm going to leave in the resources the blog post, the original blog post that was written when this perceptron was developed.

And there you can understand a bit more about the training process and if you want to go a bit more deep into that. But what happens is that that word in that context will get a classification from the number of tags, the huge number of tags, you would have as an output, the element with the highest probability for that word. So here, for example, in seashells, you would pass this through the POS tagger that you have on NLTK, and the POS tagger would give you as an output the plural noun, because it understands that this word based on the context and the word itself, for example, it ends in an S that may be a good hint that it's a plural word, that it's a plural noun.

And given that, given the context and characteristics of the word, it will output the plural noun as the right part-of-speech tag for this word. when you build the model, the objective of the model is always to classify new data that your model didn't see. Let's make this a bit more clear with just a simple model that doesn't rely on neural networks for you to understand a bit about these concepts. So, for example, we are going to train our own probabilistic models using NLTK and using this concept of unigram.

I'll also explain it here. So let's consider this word, which is the word nail that can be used as a verb or it can be used as a noun. For example, he used to nail that concept. This is a verb to nail. It's easy to nail the idea. It's also used as a verb. I broke a nail. It's used as a noun because it's a nail, from parts of the finger. So imagine that this would be fed into a model. Not only the nail would have the real classification. So someone read the text and said this is used as a verb here.

And it does that for all the other words. So he is used as a [unclear] tag, and it would give the [unclear]; used would also be used as a verb; concept would be used as a noun. And for example, the idea would also be used as a noun. So after having all that, imagine that all these sentences were tagged, you can tell your model. For example, let's focus on nail. So how many times does the word nail in my text corpus? This is what you would usually call text corpus.

How many times does the word nail appear as a verb? And as you can see, we have two over three because our word appears three times. And it is classified as a verb Two times in this case, we would say that 66 percent of the times our word nail is classified as a verb, while it is classified as a noun only one third of the time. Now, this is not really a probabilistic model because we will give the maximum probability to a specific tag. And in this case, what's the maximum probability that we have here?

Well, it's the 66 percent. So if we have new sentences to tag. Imagine now that someone would give you a whole bunch of sentences and told you I need to tag the part of speech of every sentence that I have here and two of the sentences were, for example, this. I was able to nail the experiment. Then here you would classify as verb, because in your text corpus, when you saw the word nail, it was more probable that it was a verb than a noun. And you say, OK, this is a verb.

And then you got I painted a nail. And in this case, you would also say it's a verb because you've seen it with more likelihood in the training set. You can also call this training set because this is what you are basing your decisions on. And you would say that this is a verb, although here it's used as a noun. So it's, this is wrong. Now, in our sentences, in our new sentences, we have 50 percent accuracy. One is correct. The other is wrong. So in that case, it's simple.

One divided by two, we have 50 percent accuracy. Now, let's imagine something. One of the things that we could use to classify this as a verb was to look to the word before, because if we have 'to nail', then we are certain that the nail is a verb. If we don't have 'to' before, then probably it's going to be a noun. In that case, we can fall on the concept of bigrams. And what bigrams do is not only considering the word, but also considering the preceding word to do the classification.

In this case, how many times do I see the expression 'to nail'? I see two times and they are verbs 100 percent of the time. How many times do I see 'a nail'? Well, I see it one time and it is a noun if I pass my new sentences. Now, in the bigram example, I was able to nail the experiment or classify it as a verb because right now the 'a nail', I've seen it in the sentence and it was a noun, so I can consider it confidently as a noun. I've also updated these probabilities to make it a bit more explicit.

And let's see. So, for example, the 'to nail' appears as a verb in two of the two examples that we have here. So we have two examples where we see the expression 'to nail', and we say that nail then is a verb in this case, 100 percent of the times that we see that expression. In the case of the noun, we see 'a nail' only once. We see that the nail, when it's accompanied by an 'a', is 100 percent true being a noun, because that's what you have seen in the training data.

Of course, these these are also small examples. So if it's in a larger corpus or a larger text. This is not that clear. And on the downside, when you use bigrams in your text, what you mean is that you have to find exactly the combination of words to do the classification. You can use something called the backoff. And the backoff means that when you don't find the two words at the same time, then you will fall back on a model that was trained on only a single word in this case, this one.

Even so, it enables you to improve the accuracy of your model, because in this case, if you use this to nail, to classify as a verb and nail, to classify as a noun, we would get 100 percent accuracy in our part of speech tag for the word nail. Notice that this would only classify the word nail as the verb or a noun. The 'to' would not be classified, but related to the expression 'able to', and that 'a' would be classified related to the expression 'painted a'.

OK, so this is just a simple, simple model because it just counts, basically counts the number of times you have the occurrence of the word nail in this case to nail as a verb and the occurrence of the word nail with a nail as a noun and assigns the best tag that has the highest probability seen on this training data. This is, of course, not how most of the machine learning algorithms work. This is just a simple example of a trained model that we can use.

And of course, we will develop more advanced models throughout the course. So I've also talked a bit about the accuracy and how we can evaluate the model. We will do that related to accuracy. For example, how can we measure the performance of these POS taggers? Well, we have this sentence. For example, Game of Thrones is roughly based on the storylines of... And imagine that you would tag each of the words with their POS, with a specific POS tag, and then you would compare with the real words that you have to understand if this makes sense or not.

So in this case, imagine that you would fail on the word Thrones, on the word based, on the word storylines, and on the word of. And then you would see, for example, I failed four out of 10. In this case, I've got correct six out of 10. So I consider that my accuracy is 60 percent on unseen data, on data that the model didn't see. Now, you can also do this evaluation on training data. That's normally not the best idea, as we will see. OK, final concept that we will explore here in natural language toolkit is the concept of n-grams, which basically means how you group data. For example, a unigram, in this case patients as evidence of macular degeneration.

You can consider as a unigram each word by itself. This is the unigram approach. You can consider bigrams, which is this word paired with this word, and this word paired with this one, and this one with this one, and so on. You would get these pairs of words. you can go for trigrams, which means that you consider these three words at the same time and you can go for four-grams and five-grams and so on. So it could continue until the n-grams that you want.

That's the expression of n-grams. Of course, this also has its downsides, as we will see in the section where we will explore this. OK, so we spoke about a lot of concepts here throughout the lecture, but we will explore them again step by step in our next section on natural language toolkit. Another word is when you have a full stop, you should remove that full stop from the word in that tokenizer, in the tokens.

009 Porter, Snowball and Lancaster Stemmers.en
==============================================

And welcome again to this natural language processing course. OK, so here we are going to speak about an important concept in natural language processing called stemming. Stemming is a way for us to get to the root of a specific word. Let us start with our EU definition that we have been working so far and see an example here that we have here, for example, the word members and the word member. Depending on your application, it may be interesting to consider both of these words as the same.

Remember that for a computer right now, members and member are completely different because they have different binary representations in the way that they are stored in the computer. So stemming will help us to do this, to get to the root of the word, for example, plural and singular of the same word will be stemmed into the same root, into the same member root and also other cases that we will see next that don't share exactly the same letters, but will be stemmed to the same root.

There isn't a one size fits all approach when it comes to stemming and lemmatization, which is something that we will discuss next. Some practitioners argue that stemming takes out information from your text, and that's correct. That's a fact, as soon as you reduce characters in your text, because that's indeed what you are doing. That may not be acceptable in a world where you have more computing power and also more powerful algorithms, although I argue that for more simpler models and for more baseline models, we'll discuss that in the future in this course.

But for model such as logistic regression, Naive Bayes, or models that have less capacity to deal with rare features such as neural networks, for example, stemming might be a good way to go, at least in those cases and in those examples. But of course, all of this depends on your NLP application, and you should always try to understand what the impact of stemming or not stemming a specific word, although again, this is still an ongoing discussion. I think that stemming is a good technique, particularly for reducing text and probably in the future will be used a bit less than what it is today, because the scientific developments will let you use the entire text.

But even so, it's a really relevant technique for you to learn. OK, so let's implement our stemmer and see examples of how words get stemmed. So we are going to study three types of stemmers: Lancaster, Snowball and Porter. So let me start with the last one, Porter. Porter Stemmer is one of the most widely used stemmers out there. And Porter Stemmer, what it does is basically checking common terminations of words in English and also remove most of the plurals from the words.

011 WordNet Lemmatizer.en
=========================

Everyone, and welcome again to this natural language processing course. OK, so we spoke about stemmers and how we can stem a sentence. In the beginning of this section, I've also talked to you about lemmatization. So lemmatization is similar to stemming, although it has some specific differences regarding the arguments that we need to give to the lemmatization function. So the function of lemmatization is also to obtain the root of the word. But instead of just obtaining a grammatical root, meaning a suffix with the same letters as we've seen in the stemming sentences, lemmatization gets to the morphological root of the word, meaning some origin of the word in terms of its meaning.

Don't worry if this may seem confusing, because we'll do, of course, many practical examples to understand this. Let me do here a heading called Lemmatization, and I'll give it this heading. Now let me do something, which is from nltk.stem import PorterStemmer. I'll use Porter Stemmer to compare with the lemmatizer that we are going to do. Then from nltk.stem import WordNetLemmatizer. Let's run both of these and now we are going to compare what both of these will do to a specific word.

Now the main difference between stemming and lemmatization, apart from the meaning of the root that they get, is that the WordNet lemmatizer, or the lemmatization process, needs a part of speech tag. Don't worry about part of speech, because in the next lectures, we are going to pick up a model that NLTK already has pre-developed with part of speech. That seems weird, but now let's do a change, which is giving to this lemmatize a POS, so the part of speech. So am is a verb, right, from I am, You are, he is, she is, etc.. Now we have a lemmatized version of the word am as be. So what happened here? Well, with the lemmatizer, and giving the lemmatizer the verb, the lemmatizer uses an instance of WordNet that contains metadata for a tree of words and verb relationships, for example, the verb be when it's conjugated may be am, may be is. If I change here to is, the root is exactly the same. If I change to are, the root is exactly the same. This doesn't happen with stemmers, because stemmers are extracting the suffix of some word, and in are there's no suffix called be.

So this would work in a stemmer if this word was be-are or whatever. That's not how the English language works. So that's why lemmatization is a bit different from stemming. If I don't give any POS tag, I won't be able to find what's the root of the word. But this is extremely interesting. So there are four POS tags that go in here in this WordNet lemmatizer: the a for adjective, the v that we've seen for verb, the n for noun, and also the r for adverb. Sometimes people will intertwine between the two. So when we train the model, it's super important that we don't evaluate our algorithm or the performance of our model in the data that we train, because if we are giving data to an algorithm, the algorithm will find something most of the times. But you don't know if that's something that it found will be enough to generalize in the real world.

What I mean by this is that in our example, imagine that we train a tag on legal documents and then we want to apply that post tag that we have in the legal documents to Twitter messages. It probably would have bad accuracy because the type of the language is completely different. I bet you don't have all the legal documents in the world in your training base. So in the data that you see the training model as probably that's the case, it's important that you test your algorithm on unseen data because that unseen data will tell you with more confidence how your algorithm will behave in the real world. And even after checking the unseen data, checking your performance on the unseen data, that doesn't mean that your algorithm will be good in the real world and that it won't degrade its performance.

This is a central concept in data science and algorithm training, so never, ever evaluate your model on your training data. Hello, everyone, and welcome again to this NLP course. So in this lecture, we are going to explore the concept of n-grams, so [unclear] here. So n-grams is a word that you'll hear a lot in NLP, because n-grams really translate in a way of grouping text or treating texts in a specific sentence or in a specific corpus. So you've seen our bigrams in our own POS tag training lecture. But even so, there are not only bigrams, there are trigrams and n-grams, for example.

So we don't stop at two words in conjunction. So let's start again with our Lord of the Rings quote, and I'm going to pick it up from a past lecture. So here it is. And I want to leave it here. OK, so let's now check our Lord of the Rings, quote, printing it. And here is a quote. OK, so when we are doing NLP applications, most of the applications by default treat words independently. So I cannot read the fiery are all treated as independent words. And when we are creating features or variables that we want, for example, to classify our text, we can treat them as different words.

So the fiery and the letters are not connected at all. But for some cases there are certain parts that we really want to consider two or three words together. Negation is the most easy example that I can give you when we say, for example, "I don't want". If we just look at the "want", you probably would think that that person wanted something. But if you have a don't before in the English language that "don't" is negating that "want". So if you look at the words independently, you just don't know what's the word that's before the "want" in a sentence.

Let's see if we have some negation right here in this sentence. And, for example, must not get it. If we look at the words individually, the "get" will be treated as a single word. But if we treat this as bigrams, which means that we have a continuous pair of words on our text as features, or as tokens. Imagine the tokenizer: our main tokenizer only treated one single word at a time. With word tokenizers, we can have these kinds of word tokenizers that don't treat a single word all the time, but treat multiple words.

And in this case, we will have one of those tokens that will contain the "not get". Let's see that by taking a look at the ngrams module from NLTK. and everygrams, and all of these three classes will be available for me in my environment. OK, let's run this one. As you've probably guessed, these bigrams and trigrams and everygrams receive a tokenized version of our sentence. So let's tokenized our sentence using our word_tokenize, and I'm going to import it here. You probably know this by heart. So from nltk.tokenize import word_tokenize. And let's do a tokenized version of our Lord of the Rings, quote, OK.

006 Scraping a Web Page - Errors in Request.en
==============================================

Hello, everyone, and welcome again to this course. So, small parentheses here in our scraping web pages and learning how to retrieve text data from the Web. There are certain websites that will give you some trouble when you are scraping as an example, will try to scrape my LinkedIn profile and see what we will have and if we can extract text from that data. So I have here the URL for my LinkedIn that we will visit. And as you can see, we have here my summary and we also have here some examples of my experience in multiple companies and what I've done in multiple companies.

Let's see if we can extract this using what we've learned so far, the requests library and the beautiful soup library. OK, so here we'll do our LinkedIn data and we'll use requests that get and pass our URL. Let's check what's the response? It's nine nine nine. So this is the status code [unclear]. If you try to debug why this is happening, you're probably really confused because this 999 does not relate with any 404 or any 200 that we've seen on the Wikipedia page.

And if you search for 999 on that Wikipedia page on HTTP status codes, you will not find this 999. What this means is that some websites try to block scraping on purpose. There's some legal debate out there if they can do that or not, and even some legal debate if scrapers are legal or not as of 2021. But responsible scraping is still something legal. By responsible, I mean that you don't do a lot of requests to a website because you can have impact on the performance of the website.

That's mostly the meaning of responsible. But some websites don't like their data to be scraped. LinkedIn is one of those websites. So if you see, for example, the content of this linkedin_data. We got this weird JavaScript code that basically tells you that you can't access that data. So using requests made it not possible for us to scrape this data. There are, of course, other techniques that you can use, for example, using other libraries such as Selenium, that we'll not approach in this course, but in scraping you'll probably approach all those other libraries that don't work as requests do.

But doing this simple scraping on some websites will lead to errors, and these errors will prevent you from getting the text from those websites. It's important that you understand this because probably you may do some requests to some website and you will not be able to get data from that website. And this is one of the main reasons, and probably one of the common ones is that websites deny that you make these requests in some way so that you are able to work with their data.

So even if the data is here, when we visit this website via browser, so we type this into a browser and we can check all the text that we might want to scrape if we use the requests, library, notice that the URL is exactly the same. So this URL matches the URL that we were seeing. We see that we can't scrape that. Try this with your LinkedIn to see if you can scrape some data from LinkedIn. I bet that you'll have this response 999, and then you can't work with the data from this website.

And like LinkedIn, there are multiple websites out there that will block you when you try to use requests to access that data.

001 Introduction to Word Vectors.en
===================================

Hello, everyone, and welcome again to this NLP course. So we've entered a whole new world on NLP. In this chapter, we are going to explore how we can transform words or the concept of words and sentences into numbers that will make sense mathematically for a computer. So at the moment, we've been working with strings. We've been manipulating a couple of things. We've been always trying to represent our strings as text. And we know that the computer stores that text as binary representations of the string.

But that doesn't really mean anything about the logical or semantics of a specific word. As an example, we humans would know the difference or the similarity between two words just by knowing the meaning and having the visual representation of that word in our minds. For a computer that's not that simple, because words are just representations in binary elements. An example would be if I would ask you, what's the similarity between a banana and an orange?

You would know that they are two fruits and they belong to that huge category of fruit. You'd probably know that they might be together in a sentence and they probably substitute each other in a specific sentence, such as "I ate a banana" or "I ate an orange". That concept of when words co-occur together or when they substitute themselves in a specific sentence is what we are mostly interested in when we want to represent a specific semantic logic of the word.

So you have an extremely complex human brain and we can visualize this relationship like how can we relate a banana and an orange and how they would substitute each other in a specific sentence. So what we are going to do in this section is trying to represent both words as numbers and numbers that make sense mathematically and sentences as numbers so that we can continue with our study in NLP. This is a really important step in NLP, and it's really important that you understand these concepts.

So take your time to understand them. Don't rush through the materials. And don't worry if you can't pick the examples that I'm going to show you at the first glance, because this will definitely take time. OK, so let's start first with our studying on how we can represent words as numbers.

003 Word Co-Occurence Matrices.en
=================================

So a really important concept in natural language processing is that you can represent the word by its company, so you can represent the word based on the words that are around that word. Because imagine we have here in this one, he bought a banana. So bought, banana. And then we have, for example, bought a pear. So these neighbors are similar: a pear, or bought a, and also we have here bought a banana again. And for example, imagine this carry. The carry is surrounded by they and by cherry. Ate is surrounded by they and a banana. Ate is surrounded by they and a pear.

So they are surrounded by similar words. They enter in the sentence in the same place. And that's super interesting because you can characterize, for example, this by the words that surround this word. For example, instead of having this, we can have the ate a cherry, because you also have ate around they and a, and the same goes for other words. For example, we bought a pear, to be... we bought a banana, similar to what we have in this sentence. So this concept is super, super important, which is a word can be characterized by the neighbors that that word has.

Let's see how we can implement this and see how this makes a lot of sense. Let's try to come up with a vector that should be able to represent how that word is represented by their neighbors. So we need to build this, which is here. We'd like to have counts on that. So how many times does the word a appear with banana on its neighbors in a sentence? So banana is the next word or is the previous word, and we will have here counts for every time that happens in our sentence. So first we need to tokenize each sentence that we have with a word tokenizer because we want to have what's the position of the next and of the previous word. 006 CBOW - Obtaining Word Vectors (Embeddings).en

Hello, everyone, and welcome again to this natural language processing course, so we have trained our model and now with this model that we have trained, we can retrieve word vectors. Let us again visualize the concept of the neural network and visualize the architecture. So this is another way of visualizing a single neuron. What I want you to focus on is these WS right here. So this WS are the weights. And what we are really learning on each layer of the neural network is this W1, W2 until the n,

where n is the number of columns that we have. This y hat right here is the final prediction that we had after running our neural network and using the .predict method. But if we are really learning these weights, remember that our neural network really had a hidden layer in this case. This architecture does not have any hidden layer, just has a sum function across all the nodes times, the weights of the first layer. But in our case, we have another layer that will generate and there will be weights generated between the first layer and the hidden layer and weights generated between the hidden layer and the final output layer.

So that's really where the magic is, because while our training process is going forward, these weights right here, the weights that are generated by this neural network are the word embeddings. Each layer generates its own word embedding and its own word vector, because this layer will have the size of the vocab that we have. So for each word on our vocab, a vector will be generated across our weights. Let's see how we can retrieve these weights in Python and in the neural network that we have trained.

So if we go for our model, remember that this model is a Keras model, so a neural network that we've trained, a sequential one that runs from the input until the output in this case, until the prediction and the model that we have trained using here the model.fit. And now we are extracting another set of embeddings for the same words. But now these embeddings that we have extracted are the things that are generated in this case from the hidden layer to the output layer. Now something that's really commonly done is to pick up both of the weights and divide them by two.

So the embedding of the word or the word embedding extracted will be the sum of both weights divided by two. And here we have our final embeddings. We combine both layers, combine the first set of weights and the second set of weights from the hidden to the output layer divided them by two so that we average both embeddings, and then we obtain the final word embeddings.

003 TF-IDF.en
=============

Hello, everyone, and welcome again to this NLP course. So here we'll explore this concept of TF-IDF. TF-IDF seems a bit scary at the beginning when you look at the formula, but it's really not. It's super simple to understand. And I hope that during this lecture you'll get the intuition behind it. First, let's look at the formula of the TF-IDF, and one of the ways to convey this formula. You may see some other formulas that derive from this formula that may have small changes, but even so, the intuition is always the same.

Behind the concept of the TF-IDF is to score in a specific document words that are more rare in the whole corpus and that occur more frequently on the document, better than other words that are more common throughout the document. As an example, the word the should have a low TF-IDF score for all the tweets that we have on our base, if the word the is present in all the tweets and it probably is present with some relevance in our tweets. So this tactic really weighs

what's the [unclear] word, if I can call it in this way, of a specific tweet or of a specific document? Let's implement this bit by bit. So the first part is the number of occurrences of the word i in the tweet. I'm always referring to the word tweet because this is the corpus that we are working with. But you can translate this to book to sentence to page to document whatever you want, as long as it has text and then this will multiply. This term will multiply for the log of the number of documents that you have divided by the number of documents that contain the word that you are checking and you check that the more rare the word is across all your documents, the higher this value will be.

Let's check that in code and implement that really quickly. OK, so I'm again on the tweets and I'll go for the tweets, texts and access this "Forest fire near La Ronge Sask. Canada". So I'm going to use as a base the first 10 tweets, our tweets.text, just as simplification. Now let us go for the word fire. What's the TF-IDF value for the word fire if we had this tweet as a target, and our base would be these 10 tweets? Let's start with the first part of the formula that should be this bit right here.

So I want the number of occurrences of fire in this tweet. This is just one. Right, because fire is only present one time in my documents. Now, second part we can have I'll import math here, and then I'm going to have math.log, and then I'm going to have a division here. First part is the length of my corpus, in this case, 10. Now, the second part or the denominator is the number of times fire appears in my tweets. So I'll see from all the tweets. I'll go here and I'll do a cool thing, which is .str.contains.

And here what I'm trying to check is if fire is contained on these tweets. So fire is present. If I sum all of this in five tweets. So here I'll have five tweets. Now I can multiply this and the TF-IDF score for the word fire is 0.69. What would happen if the word fire was less present on my corpus? If the word fire only appeared in two tweets, my TF-IDF score would immediately be higher. This conveys the meaning that the word fire for this tweet will be extremely relevant because it's present here and it's not present in the rest of the corpus.

Weighing in some way more for this tweet than the other words that are more present in the rest of the corpus. This is interesting. Now, another way to raise the score is, of course, if the word fire would appear three times in my document, it would even make this word more relevant to this tweet in specific. It was that easy. So this is the TF-IDF implementation. Sometimes it seems more complicated than that. It's really that simple. So you can manipulate here, for example, if we have more documents, a hundred documents our TF-IDF is even higher because the word is even more rare in our corpus, as our corpus is not 10 documents now and it's a hundred.

So you can manipulate this formula back and forth. You'll understand that the two sides will mimic the importance of a specific word for a specific tweet or for a specific document in a corpus. Now, again, we don't need to do this by ourself. We have an implementation in sklearn that's really cool, which is from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import TfidfVectorizer. So columns are equal to our tfidf, we again have this cool get_feature_names that enables us to get the vocab.

So there you go. And as you can see, one curious thing is that the TfidfVectorizer approach is normalized and we don't have values higher than one. So this TF IDF implementation in particular is normalized because this is proven a lot of times that it's better to have these values normalized between zeros and ones. And that's what this implementation does. Again, one thing, you can use the min_df here. I'm going to pass, for example, 0.005. Of course, as the score is directly tied with the relevance of the word in a specific tweet of the presence or rarity of a word in a specific tweet.

If you put a too high threshold, your TF-IDF scores will be a bit weird. Even so, let's use 0.005 here as a min_df. And I'm going to pass all our tweets instead of only this one. This is not considered in our formula that we've seen before behind the intuition, but it's something that also this implementation of sklearn does, which is there's also this normalization between when the tweet is smaller. So we have fewer words in your document and this word appears with relevance. logistic regression. Logistic regression is then a classifier that can separate between two classes, such as ones and zeros. In this case, it can separate between positive and negative reviews. And on the contrary, if you have those words appearing less times in your review, then there is less probability that they are positive. For example, if you have the classes separated in this way, the logistic regression will have a hard time to draw a simple line between all these examples. There's no chance in two dimensions in these two dimensions, only that the logistic regression can draw a line that will perfectly separate the positive from the negative classes.

This is only possible with a hyperplane, or dealing with more advanced stuff, but this algorithm, logistic regression, can't do that. But imagine that in the classification example, the loss, what we call the loss is the error of your classification. So imagine that you give a specific weight randomly to that feature worst and your classification error will be here on the starting point.

So you have a lot of error given the weight or the all the possible weights that that word can have to classify your target to classify the review as positive or negative. How these algorithms learn is that they are trying to descend in some way to this point, at some point where the weight will minimize the error that you will have with your classification. So they work iteratively. They will understand that they are making errors on the prediction, given the weights, and then they will try to adjust the weights for each of the features in this case, for each of our 6000 features, that it gets the best possible weights for its classification so that when you pass a new sentence on this algorithm, the probability that you are going to have on that sentence, being positive or not, is going to be the best one found on the training examples.

Of course, this may not happen so easily, and it's hard to make an algorithm generalize for several texts, as we will see. But this is the intuition behind the training of the algorithm. So imagine that you only have a feature or a variable. You give a weight to that variable, and then you iteratively try to find what's the best point, or weight, for that variable to get the minimum error possible. So to get your algorithm to fail less and less, in this case, to be able to predict the target that you want to predict.

In our case, in our practical example, what we are doing is trying to predict the sentiment of a specific movie review. OK, and that's approximately how these algorithms work and learn. They assign weights to try to find the best way to minimize the error. And then they go forward when they reach that point of convergence, meaning that they found the minimum possible with the variables that they have. Of course, remember, this algorithm only is able to deal with those linearly separable classes and this may not happen.

So the sigmoid function basically is this function of one over one, plus the letter E, in this case, the Euler's number raised to the power of minus Z, where Z is the resulting value from the weight that you have assigned to the variable times the value of the variable itself.

In this case, the 1.2 that we assign as a weight, times one, that will be 1.2. And as you can see, the sigmoid function is always between zero and one, and it behaves as we should expect in the probability. So the higher the value, the more probable we assign this example to class one, the smaller the value, the more probability we assign to the fact that it belongs to class zero. OK, so this is what we call the sigmoid function. Let's now pass our examples through this formula.

009 Train and Test Split.en
===========================

Everyone, and welcome again to this natural language processing course. OK, so we are ready to train our model. We already have all the concepts that we need. So let's go forward before training. Let's also separate some of our training base into different data. Why am I doing this? Well, this is basically to guarantee that we are evaluating our model in some data that the model never saw. This is an extremely important concept in machine learning, in data science, and it is called basically model evaluation.

Ideally, you would even have a training, a test and a validation sets or three completely different sets, one to evaluate the training process and another one to evaluate in a completely different data set that I would even recommend. That doesn't even come from the same type, or be from the same type of text. But even so here, it will be sufficient for us to use only two data sets. The training one, the one that is fed to the training model and is use to compute the weights and the test set, which will be one where we will evaluate the model.

This is extremely important because you want your algorithm and your NLP classification model to be able to generalize in the real world. When you do an algorithm, what you really want is that your model serves some future data. For example, if you want to evaluate new reviews that someone will give you, you will not know the type of that review beforehand. So you want to have good accuracy on data that your model never seen, because if you only work with data in the past, believe me, that your model will find something that will give you a good result.

But this is an extremely important concept in machine learning. Never, ever evaluate your model on the training set. We've already discussed this in other sections, but I really think that it's really important that we focus on it again. So what I'm going to do here is then for X_full, I'm going to do the following, which is going from the first until the fiftieth review. So this will take out the first 50 reviews that are all positive. And I'm going to take out, I'm going to append the last, in this case from the 1950

until the last review. so let me just confirm that this has indeed 50 reviews this way and from the thousand nine hundred and fifty and it looks like we have 50 reviews and these are negative reviews. This is just for our test set to contain both classes. That's also important. Don't only use one class to evaluate the model, of course. So I'm going to do this and I'm going to call this X_test. This will be data that our model, our training process will never see.

So to optimize the weights, the model will never look at this data. It's as if this data or these reviews never existed. 011 Model Regularization.en
===========================

So we can solve this using something called regularization, regularization is something that you can use in a logistic regression, for example, so that your model tries to generalize a bit better, not get too much excited with the training data. Let's call it in this way. So what your model will do is that it will penalize some of the weights that it's learning, to control for this overfit. So it's trying to avoid to be so perfect in a training set, knowing that by doing so, it'll probably have a higher [unclear] in the test set.

So how can we do that? Well, here I'm going to do logreg_reg, and here I'm going to pass a parameter called penalty is equal to 'l2', and a C, which will be... This is basically just parameters of regularization that I'm using, but what happens is that the C, the lower the value of the C, the more regularization you are applying to your model, meaning the less extreme you are trying your model to be. So it's as if you are moderating your model using this parameter.

Let's see the impact. So I'm going to do logreg_reg, and here I'm going to do a fit, of course. So let's fit here, and [unclear]. It's L2. Sorry about that. I didn't confirm the argument first and how it's supposed to work and our process works. Now what I'm going to do is also to do a prediction or in this case a Y new based on my new regularized regression. Exactly in the same data. And I want the accuracy score and you'll see that will be lower than 100 percent.

As you can see, this algorithm now scores 88 percent on the training data. Well, let's see the results on the testing data in this case. If I go here and will predict based on this, what we will have then is having this accuracy of our y_pred_test. Is 85 percent, so even lower, this seems that your model is worse and it may be, but the difference between the training and the testing that probably makes your model a bit better going to the future because you're not in such a big overfit.

So when you have a big difference between the training and the test set, again, it's normally some yellow flag or at least an orange flag that you should look at before putting your model into production. Although the test set accuracy is less when you regularize normally with the delta between the train and test set accuracy being lower, that would mean that your model should be a bit more stable, at least theoretically.
